# Exact scope and disposition

The unchanged original P13.2 is **not solved in this paper**. No intrinsic sufficiency or actual inverse existence is claimed in general dimension. The domain remains every normal good polarized affine cone over every algebraically closed trivially valued characteristic-zero field, with every Reeb field, including irrational fields. The carrier remains the fixed compact space $K=X_0^T$, and every MA below is the genuine affine-cone probability. The final flag is `candidate=false`.

The supplied full P13.1 mathematical proof, explicitly accepted with its original PASS review, is the sole accepted new theorem. The supplied round71, round70, and root79 drafts are not premises. This paper independently derives the finite-target and approximation assertions from the accepted calculus, and adds a linear fixed-ray homogeneous polynomial and a uniform bounded-energy target-integration estimate. It also reconstructs explicit strict supports, rational affine approximations at logarithmic poles, a normal-ring localization lemma, and a retraction argument for carrier envelopes. Those auxiliaries do not establish the normal singular rational envelope base.

The precise new estimate is as follows. Put $p=\dim X-1\ge1$ and $\alpha=2^{-p}$. For a Radon probability $\mu$ on $K$, define the finite-test dual energy
$$
 L(\mu)=\sup_{w\in H(\xi)}\left(E(w)-\int_Kw\,d\mu\right).
 \tag{1}
$$
For $L(\mu)<\infty$, let $\psi$ be an actual normalized finite-energy potential, with deficit
$$d_\psi=L(\mu)-E(\psi)+\int_K\psi\,d\mu.$$
Constants denoted $C_p$ depend only on $p$ and may be enlarged between displays. In particular,, for every actual normalized $\phi\in\mathcal E^1$,
$$
 \left|\int_K\phi\,d(\mathrm{MA}(\psi)-\mu)\right|
 \le C_p\sqrt{d_\psi}\,J(\phi)^\alpha
       \big(J(\phi)+J(\psi)+B_0\big)^{1/2-\alpha},
 \qquad B_0=1+\frac{L(\mu)+1}{2a_p},\quad a_p=\frac p{4^{p+1}}.
 \tag{2}
$$
Here $J(\phi)=-E(\phi)$ after normalization at the trivial valuation. If $p=1$, the last factor has exponent zero and is interpreted as one. Formula (2) proves uniform convergence of **signed target integrals** on bounded-$J$ sets along a maximizing sequence. It also proves convergence of the dual energies of its genuine output measures. It does not assert $L^1$ continuity along arbitrary weakly convergent nonmonotone potential nets, nor compactness or actual membership of a potential limit.

# Accepted calculus and exact normalization

Let $n=\dim X\ge1$, $p=n-1$, and write $\rho=\rho_\xi$, $V=V_\xi$. The actual continuous class is the local-uniform closure of finite primary homogeneous maxima
$$u=\max_i\frac{\lambda_i-v(f_i)}{\langle\xi,\mathrm{wt}(f_i)\rangle}.$$
PSH consists of nontrivial decreasing CPSH-net limits. Potentials in this paper are restrictions of those classes, not a replacement definition on $K$. The accepted theorem supplies genuine positive mixed products, integration by parts, the fixed-ray Hodge inequality, bounded local-uniform continuity, weighted decreasing-net continuity, and actual convexity and maximum stability.

The conventions are
$$
 \nu(u_1,\ldots,u_p)=V^{-1}dd^cu_1\cdots dd^cu_p\,dd^c\rho^+,
 \quad \rho^+=\max(\rho,0),\quad \nu(K)=1,
 \tag{3}
$$
$$
 E(u)=\frac1{nV}\sum_{j=0}^p\int_Ku\,(dd^cu)^j(dd^c\rho)^{p-j}dd^c\rho^+.
 \tag{4}
$$
The reference restricts to zero on $K$. On unbounded finite-energy potentials, these expressions mean the actual fixed-field weighted decreasing limits. The accepted outer energy is
$$F(g)=\sup_{w\in\mathrm{CPSH},\ w\le g}E(w),\qquad
 F^\downarrow(h)=\inf_{g\in C(K),\ g\ge h}F(g). \tag{5}$$
It agrees with the existing energy on every actual PSH potential and is decreasing-net continuous. For a fixed uniformly dense direction lattice $\mathcal T\subset C(K)$, each $f\in\mathcal T$ has actual slope-$A$ auxiliary representatives $f=a-b$ with $a/A,b/A\in\mathrm{CPSH}(\xi)$. Both signs of the accepted estimate are
$$
 |F^\downarrow(u+tf)-E(u)-t\int_Kf\,d\mathrm{MA}(u)|
 \le C_f t^2,\qquad C_f=2pA(1+A\tau)^{p-1}\|f\|_K,\quad |t|\le\tau.
 \tag{6}
$$
This is an outer functional, with no envelope-attainment identity assumed.

We also deduce the reference identity $\mathrm{MA}(\rho)=\delta_{v_{\rm triv}}$. Rationally, the zero-weight product model on the normal integral projective quotient has one central component of multiplicity one and mixed intersection equal to the projective volume. Its projective probability is the trivial-valuation Dirac mass. The accepted radial Gauss lift gives $v_{\rm triv}$ on $K$. Irrational references are bounded local-uniform limits of rational references; accepted genuine current continuity gives the same Dirac measure. Thus normalized $u$ has
$$u(v_{\rm triv})=\sup_Ku=0,\qquad u\le0,\qquad J(u)=-E(u). \tag{7}$$

For a rational field $\eta=s\eta_e$, the accepted effective supported grading has gcd one. On a suitable degree-$d$ Veronese quotient with actual ample bundle $L_d$, the calibration is
$$
 V_\eta=s^{-n}d^{1-n}(L_d^p),\quad \omega_\eta=L_d/(sd),\quad
 (\omega_\eta^p)=sV_\eta,\quad E_\eta=E_{L_d}/(sd). \tag{8}
$$
The finite map has generic degree $d$, the $n$ curvature factors each contribute $1/(sd)$, and the radial Gauss current has one unit mass. The full-ring signed mean has $E=(n+1)S/n$. We retain these factors throughout; there is no replacement by an unnormalized projective measure.

# Reconstruction of the finite-target criterion

For a decreasing bounded-above usc net $h_i\downarrow h$ on compact Hausdorff $K$ and a Radon probability $\mu$,
$$\int h_i\,d\mu\downarrow\int h\,d\mu. \tag{9}$$
Here the integral of a bounded-above usc function is the infimum of its continuous-majorant integrals. For bounded functions this follows by regular approximation of level sets; truncation below gives the extended version. If $g\ge h$ is continuous, the increasing open sets $\{h_i<g+\epsilon\}$ cover $K$. A finite subcover and directedness give $h_i\le g+\epsilon$ eventually. Taking the infimum over $g$ proves (9), including value $-\infty$. No sequence-only monotone convergence is substituted for nets.

Uniform $H$ approximation and actual energy continuity equate (1) with its CPSH supremum. If $L(\mu)<\infty$ and $u_i\downarrow u\in\mathcal E^1$ is an actual defining net, then
$$\int u_i\,d\mu\ge E(u_i)-L(\mu).$$
Equation (9) and decreasing energy continuity imply $\int u\,d\mu\ge E(u)-L(\mu)>-\infty$. All finite-energy potentials are therefore $\mu$-integrable, and
$$
 L(\mu)=\sup_{u\in\mathcal E^1}\left(E(u)-\int u\,d\mu\right)=E^\vee(\mu).
 \tag{10}
$$
When the bounded-test supremum is infinite, the E1 supremum is already infinite since it contains those bounded tests. This establishes equality as an extended functional as well.

Conversely, assume every actual E1 potential is $\mu$-integrable. On normalized $u$ put
$$b_l=-\int u\,d\nu(u^{[l]},\rho^{[p-l]})\ge0,\quad 0\le l\le p.$$
Weighted mixed continuity gives finiteness, the reference Dirac gives $b_0=0$, and $J(u)=n^{-1}\sum_l b_l$. The actual segment $u_t=tu+(1-t)\rho$, $0\le t\le1$, has on $K$ the restriction $tu$. Expanding the actual positive products gives
$$
 J(u_t)=\frac1n\sum_{l=1}^p
 t\sum_{j=l}^p{j\choose l}t^l(1-t)^{j-l}b_l
 \le2^nt^2J(u). \tag{11}
$$
Indeed $t^{l+1}\le t^2$ and $\sum_{j=l}^p{j\choose l}={n\choose l+1}\le2^n$. The expansion first holds for CPSH and passes through actual fixed-field weighted decreasing limits.

There is $B<\infty$ with $\int(-u)\,d\mu\le B$ on normalized $J(u)\le1$. Otherwise choose $u_j$ in that class with integrals at least $2^j$. The actual restrictions $w_m=\sum_{j\le m}2^{-j}u_j$, with the missing weight supplied by the reference off $K$, decrease, have value zero at $v_{\rm triv}$, and have energy at least $-1$ by concavity. Their actual nontrivial decreasing PSH limit is E1, but its negative integral is infinite. This contradicts the hypothesis. For $J(u)\ge2^{-n}$ take $t=(2^nJ(u))^{-1/2}$ in (11). For smaller $J$, use $B$. Thus
$$\int(-u)\,d\mu\le B+B\sqrt{2^nJ(u)},\qquad
 L(\mu)\le B+2^{n-2}B^2<\infty. \tag{12}$$
This independently reconstructs the finite-test integrability criterion on every original cone. It describes the finite-energy **target class**, without image sufficiency.

The accepted normalized root bijections are onto actual E1 classes and obey $u/c\le\mathcal R u\le u/C\le0$. Integrability of all source potentials consequently implies integrability of every target potential; the inverse gives the converse. Hence the finite-energy measure class is independent of Reeb field on the same $K$. No strong-topology assertion about roots is involved.

Fixed-ray concavity and first variation give the genuine tangent inequality
$$E(w)\le E(u)+\int(w-u)\,d\mathrm{MA}(u). \tag{13}$$
Raw simultaneous decreasing approximation and weighted mixed continuity extend it from continuous potentials to E1. Therefore every genuine output $\nu=\mathrm{MA}(u)$ has
$$L(\nu)=E(u)-\int u\,d\nu<\infty. \tag{14}$$
The printed opposite tangent sign in A, LF1249, is not used.

# Genuine approximation and midpoint Cauchyness

For continuous $g$ and every feasible $w\le g$ in (5),
$$E(w)-\int g\,d\mu\le E(w)-\int w\,d\mu\le L(\mu).$$
Thus $F(g)-\int g\,d\mu\le L(\mu)$. A decreasing net of continuous majorants of a bounded-above, integrable usc $h$ exists: minima make it directed, and separation of a point from a closed usc level set proves that its infimum is $h$. Equation (9) and outer decreasing continuity give
$$F^\downarrow(h)-\int h\,d\mu\le L(\mu). \tag{15}$$
Use (15) on $h=u+tf$ and the lower side of (6). Taking $t=s$ and $t=-s$ proves
$$
 \left|\int f\,d(\mathrm{MA}(u)-\mu)\right|\le d_u/s+C_fs,
 \qquad 0<s\le\tau. \tag{16}
$$
Choose actual $u_j\in H(\xi)$ with $d_j<j^{-2}$ and normalize by constants. For $s=1/j$, (16) gives error at most $(1+C_f)/j$ on $f\in\mathcal T$. Uniform approximation of any continuous test adds at most $2\|f-f_m\|$, since both measures are probabilities. Hence the **genuine** $\mathrm{MA}(u_j)$ converge weakly to $\mu$. The sequence comes from a real supremum, not an enumeration of all field elements. Deficit zero on an already genuine E1 potential gives the equation by both signs and density. This proves optimality of an actual maximizer, not attainment.

For $p\ge1$, put $z=u-v$ and, first for continuous same-field potentials,
$$ B_j=-V^{-1}\int z\,dd^cz\,(dd^cu)^j(dd^cv)^{p-1-j}dd^c\rho^+\ge0,
 \qquad 0\le j<p. \tag{17}$$
The accepted Hodge inequality supplies the sign. Difference of powers and integration by parts give
$$ I(u,v)=\int(u-v)\,d(\mathrm{MA}(v)-\mathrm{MA}(u))=\sum_{j=0}^{p-1}B_j. \tag{18}$$
For $g(t)=E(v+tz)$, genuine segment differentiation gives
$$-g''(t)=p\sum_{j=0}^{p-1}{p-1\choose j}t^j(1-t)^{p-1-j}B_j.$$
The midpoint Green kernel is $\frac12\min(t,1-t)$. On $[1/4,3/4]$ it is at least $1/8$, every monomial is at least $4^{-(p-1)}$, each binomial is at least one, and the interval length is $1/2$. Consequently
$$E((u+v)/2)-(E(u)+E(v))/2\ge a_p I(u,v),\quad a_p=p/4^{p+1}. \tag{19}$$
Each expression is a finite combination of accepted weighted mixed integrals. Actual raw decreasing CPSH approximants therefore pass the identity and inequality to E1. The actual midpoint deficit is nonnegative, giving
$$ I(u,v)\le(d_u+d_v)/(2a_p). \tag{20}$$
Every maximizing sequence is I-Cauchy modulo constants. Pairing normalized $u$ with the reference and using its deficit $L$ gives
$$ J(u)\le I(u,0)\le(d_u+L)/(2a_p). \tag{21}$$
The first inequality follows from the two nonnegative tangent deficits summing to $I$, with the reference deficit $J(u)$.

For completeness, a genuine-limit MA estimate follows directly from Hodge. Represent $f\in\mathcal T$ by slope-$A$ $a-b$. With $M_j=(dd^cu)^j(dd^cv)^{p-1-j}dd^c\rho^+$, Cauchy--Schwarz bounds the $j$th difference term by $\sqrt{Q_j(f)B_j}$, where
$$Q_j(f)=-V^{-1}\int f\,dd^cf\,M_j\le2A\|f\|_K.$$
Each positive product with $a$ or $b$ has mass $A$, by scaling the accepted mixed probability. Summing and using finite Cauchy--Schwarz gives
$$ |\int f\,d(\mathrm{MA}(u)-\mathrm{MA}(v))|
 \le\sqrt{2Ap\|f\|_K I(u,v)}. \tag{22}$$
Weighted decreasing continuity extends this to E1. Thus an actual E1 I-limit of the maximizing sequence would realize $\mu$. No such actual limit is obtained from (20).

# A genuinely linear fixed-ray polynomial

We now construct the finite linear algebra needed for the new bound; we do not apply the synthetic inverse theory.

Let $\mathcal D$ be the real vector space spanned by restrictions of actual CPSH potentials at this one fixed $\xi$, including constants. It is uniformly dense because it contains $\mathcal T$. Each $d\in\mathcal D$ has a unique zero-slope equivariant extension $\widetilde d(v)=d(s_\xi(v))$ to the torified punctured cone, then by torification to the punctured cone. It is a finite linear combination of genuine CPSH differences. Its distributional $dd^c$ is well defined, and all products below are finite linear combinations of the accepted bounded positive products. This uses one fixed linear class, not nonlinear root maps.

Put $\mathcal V=\mathbb R\times\mathcal D$ and project onto $\Theta=\mathbb R$ by the first coordinate. For $x=(s,d)$ set $\Omega_x=s\,dd^c\rho+dd^c\widetilde d$ and define the homogeneous polynomial of degree $p+1$
$$
 H(s,d)=\sum_{l=0}^p\frac{{p\choose l}}{l+1}s^{p-l}
 V^{-1}\int_K d\,(dd^c\widetilde d)^l(dd^c\rho)^{p-l}dd^c\rho^+,
 \qquad \mathcal F=-H. \tag{23}
$$
The primitive form is $H(s,d)=\int_0^1V^{-1}\int d(sdd^c\rho+tdd^c\widetilde d)^pdd^c\rho^+\,dt$. Expanding (4) and using $\sum_{j=l}^p{j\choose l}={p+1\choose l+1}$ proves $H(1,u)=E(u)$.

Integration by parts makes the $l+1$ differentiated copies of $d$ equal, hence for a zero-slope direction $f$,
$$\partial_d H(s,d)[f]=V^{-1}\int f\,\Omega_x^p dd^c\rho^+. \tag{24}$$
Polarization of this finite polynomial gives a symmetric $(p+1)$-linear pairing. For two zero-slope directions $a,b$ and $x_2,\ldots,x_p$,
$$
 \mathcal F\big((0,a),(0,b),x_2,\ldots,x_p\big)
 =-\frac1{p+1}V^{-1}\int a\,dd^c\widetilde b\,
        \Omega_{x_2}\cdots\Omega_{x_p}dd^c\rho^+. \tag{25}
$$
The coefficient follows by differentiating twice: $\partial^2\mathcal F=-p\int a\,dd^cb\,\Omega^{p-1}/V$, whereas a degree-$(p+1)$ polarization contributes $(p+1)p$.

Take the convex cone
$$\mathcal P=\{(s,d):s>0,\ d/s\in\mathrm{CPSH}(\xi)|_K\}
              \ \cup\ \{(0,c):c\in\mathbb R\}. \tag{26}$$
Positive combinations stay in it by accepted actual convexity and translation. The curvature of $(0,c)$ is zero. For all positive slots in (25), the fixed-ray Hodge index gives
$$a^2\cdot x_2\cdots x_p\ge0\qquad(a\in\mathcal V_0,\ x_i\in\mathcal P). \tag{27}$$
These are precisely the surjective linear projection, homogeneous polynomial, convex cone, and quadratic sign hypotheses of D Appendix A. No nice order on a global space $Z$, positive cohomology cone, ample-class identification, model axiom, topology of a generated PSH cone, or synthetic inverse theorem is inferred. They are not needed for this finite algebra application.

For $x=(1,u)$, $y=(1,v)$, its polynomial deficit is
$$\delta(x,y)=\mathcal F(x)-\mathcal F(y)-\langle\mathcal F'(y),x-y\rangle
             =E(v)-E(u)+\int(u-v)\,d\mathrm{MA}(v). \tag{28}$$
The nonnegative coefficients in its expansion give
$$I(u,v)/(p+1)\le\delta(x,y)\le pI(u,v)/(p+1),\quad
 \delta(x,y)+\delta(y,x)=I(u,v). \tag{29}$$
On normalized $u$, $\delta((1,u),(1,0))=J(u)$.

# Checking the polynomial Hölder estimate and its source application

We state the relevant consequence of D Theorem A.3, and check its proof mechanism so that the source use is not an analogy. For a fixed fiber, define
$$q(x,y)=\max_{0\le j<p}(x-y)^2y^jx^{p-1-j}.$$
The positive coefficient expansion and binomial theorem give $q\asymp\delta\asymp(x-y)^2((x+y)/2)^{p-1}$, with constants depending only on $p$.

Write $w=(x+y)/2$, $A=q(x,y)$ and $B=\max(q(x,z),q(y,z))$. For $b_j=(x-y)^2z^jw^{p-1-j}$, Cauchy--Schwarz and $2w-x=y\in\mathcal P$ give
$$b_0\le C_pA,\qquad b_{j+1}\le b_j+C_p\sqrt{b_jB},\qquad
 b_{p-1}\le C_pA^{2^{1-p}}B^{1-2^{1-p}}. \tag{30}$$
The last inequality follows by induction when $A\le B$; when $A\ge B$, the triangle inequality for the seminorm with positive slots gives $b_{p-1}\le4B$. To justify the bound used in the recurrence, convexity of $\delta(\cdot,z)$ gives $q(z,w)\le C_pB$. This convexity itself follows from the positive quadratic sign (27). Thus no quasi-triangle inequality has been assumed before its proof.

Using (30) on $x-z$ and $y-z$ with positive slot $w$ gives
$$q(x,y)\le C_pT^{2^{1-p}}\max(q(x,y),T)^{1-2^{1-p}},\quad
 T=\max(q(x,z),q(y,z)).$$
If $q(x,y)>T$, absorb its indicated power; otherwise the desired bound is already true. Hence $q(x,y)\le C'_pT$. Averaging finitely many positive slots and using this quasi-triangle inequality gives the general mixed estimate
$$|(x_0-y_0)(x_1-y_1)z_2\cdots z_p|
 \le C_p\delta(x_0,y_0)^\alpha\delta(x_1,y_1)^\alpha M^{1-2\alpha},
 \quad \alpha=2^{-p},\quad M=\max_\zeta\delta(\zeta,x_*). \tag{31}$$
For the equal-pair case this is (30), after bounding distances to the average of the $z_i$ by the reference distances; the unequal-pair case follows by Cauchy--Schwarz. Expanding $x_0^p-y_0^p$ and applying Cauchy--Schwarz once more, the first quadratic factor is comparable with $\delta(x_0,y_0)$ and (31) bounds the other. Thus
$$
 |\langle\mathcal F'(x_0)-\mathcal F'(y_0),x_1-y_1\rangle|
 \le C_p\delta(x_0,y_0)^{1/2}\delta(x_1,y_1)^\alpha M^{1/2-\alpha}.
 \tag{32}
$$
For $p=1$ this is the direct quadratic Cauchy--Schwarz inequality. These are the pertinent proof bodies of D Appendix A, LF2053--2235; (30) explicitly retains the square root in the recurrence. The source's implicit constants are not replaced by a falsely exact numerical constant.

With (24), (29), and reference $x_*=(1,0)$, (32) becomes the genuine cone bound
$$
 \left|\int(u-v)\,d(\mathrm{MA}(w)-\mathrm{MA}(z))\right|
 \le C_p I(w,z)^{1/2}I(u,v)^\alpha
       \max\{J(u),J(v),J(w),J(z)\}^{1/2-\alpha}
 \tag{33}
$$
for normalized actual continuous potentials. Constants disappear from the measure difference. Raw decreasing approximation extends (33) to E1: subtracting the decreasing values at $v_{\rm triv}$ need not give a decreasing net, but each weighted integral differs from its raw decreasing counterpart by just that convergent constant. Energies and $I$ therefore converge by accepted weighted continuity. All products and all limits have one fixed irrational or rational $\xi$. The constants depend only on $p$.

# Uniform target integrals and output dual energies

Choose normalized maximizing $w_k\in H$ with $d_k\to0$ and $d_k\le1$. Equation (21) gives $J(w_k)\le B_0$. In (33), take $u=\phi$, $v=0$, $w=\psi$, $z=w_k$. From (20) and (29),
$$
 |\int\phi\,d(\mathrm{MA}(\psi)-\mathrm{MA}(w_k))|
 \le C_p\sqrt{d_\psi+d_k}\,J(\phi)^\alpha
       (J(\phi)+J(\psi)+B_0)^{1/2-\alpha}. \tag{34}
$$
For continuous $\phi$, weak convergence from (16) lets $k\to\infty$, proving (2). The order of limits for unbounded $\phi$ is essential. First approximate it by its actual decreasing CPSH net, normalize each approximant, and prove the target estimate for each fixed approximant by sending $k\to\infty$. Then send the defining net to its limit. The fixed target uses (9)--(10); the fixed measure $\mathrm{MA}(\psi)$ uses accepted weighted continuity. The energies converge, and the subtracted normalization constants tend to zero. This proves (2) on all actual E1, with no unjustified passage of an unbounded varying test through weak measure convergence.

In particular, for $J(\phi)\le B$ and $d_j\le1$,
$$
 \sup_{J(\phi)\le B}\left|\int\phi\,d(\mathrm{MA}(w_j)-\mu)\right|
 \le C_p\sqrt{d_j}\,B^\alpha(B+2B_0)^{1/2-\alpha}. \tag{35}
$$
All tests are normalized. For $B=0$ the conclusion is obtained by the same zero-distance inequality, rather than dividing by zero. These are signed integrals. Neither (35) nor decreasing continuity alone proves absolute $L^1$ continuity along an arbitrary weak potential net.

Putting $\phi=w_j$ in (2), the genuine tangent identity (14) yields
$$
 |L(\mathrm{MA}(w_j))-L(\mu)|
 \le d_j+C_p\sqrt{d_j}\,B_0^{1/2}. \tag{36}
$$
Thus these outputs converge to $\mu$ weakly **and** in dual energy. This is an actual measure conclusion, not a potential realization.

Also take $w=w_j$, $z=0$ in (33) and then send $j\to\infty$, using the same continuous-test-first, decreasing-test-second order. For normalized $u,v\in\mathcal E^1$ this gives
$$
 |\int(u-v)\,d\mu|
 \le C_p I(u,v)^\alpha B_0^{1/2}
       (B_0+J(u)+J(v))^{1/2-\alpha}. \tag{37}
$$
Consequently the target integrals of a normalized maximizing sequence are Cauchy. Since $E(w_j)-\int w_j\,d\mu\to L(\mu)$, its normalized energies also converge to a finite value. This strengthens bounded energy, but still supplies no actual potential.

# Explicit strict supports with infinite coordinates

The following auxiliary reconstruction is independent of the unreviewed local/global bridge. On $\mathbb T^N=(\mathbb R\cup\{+\infty\})^N$, choose an integer $a\ge\log(N+1)$ and define
$$\chi_N(t)=a^{-1}\log\left(1+\sum_{i=1}^Ne^{-at_i}\right),\quad e^{-a\infty}=0. \tag{38}$$
It is continuous and convex on finite coordinates, decreases in each coordinate, and lies between $\max(0,-t_i)$ and that maximum plus one. At $t$, let $I$ be its finite coordinates and let $p_i=e^{-at_i}/(1+\sum_{i\in I}e^{-at_i})$. The real support
$$\ell_t(s)=\chi_N(t)-\sum_{i\in I}p_i(s_i-t_i) \tag{39}$$
has coefficient zero on every infinite coordinate. If a finite supported coordinate becomes infinite, its value is $-\infty$. The convex log-sum inequality gives $\chi_N\ge\ell_t$, with equality exactly at $s=t$: on the finite face strict convexity follows from the extra positive constant term, and making a formerly infinite coordinate finite increases $\chi_N$ strictly.

For every neighborhood $U$ of $t$, there is $\delta>0$ with $\chi_N-\ell_t\ge\delta$ outside $U$. Along a nonzero recession vector in the finite coordinates,
$$\max(0,-v_i:i\in I)+\sum_{i\in I}p_iv_i>0,$$
because every $p_i>0$ and $\sum p_i<1$. Thus a bounded difference sublevel confines the finite supported coordinates. It also bounds every other coordinate below, since a coordinate tending to $-\infty$ sends $\chi_N$ to $+\infty$. Its closure is compact in the extended product; at a supported coordinate $+\infty$ the difference tends to $+\infty$. The continuous difference on the remaining compact sublevel has the singleton zero set $\{t\}$. Compactness proves the gap, including the case $I$ empty.

Actual rational AFS approximation is explicit. Put $z_0=1$, $z_i=e^{-at_i}$ and $B_m={m+N\choose N}$. The multinomial expansion shows
$$
 q_m(t)=\max_{|\beta|=m}\left\{-\sum_{i=1}^N\frac{\beta_i}{m}t_i
          +\frac{\log{m\choose\beta}}{am}\right\}
 \le\chi_N(t)\le q_m(t)+\frac{\log B_m}{am}. \tag{40}
$$
This holds uniformly on the whole extended product. A term with positive $\beta_i$ at $t_i=\infty$ is $-\infty$; $\beta_0=m$ supplies a zero floor. Round intercepts upward rationally, with error at most $\epsilon_j/4$, and choose $m_j$ with the other error at most $\epsilon_j/4$. For rational $\epsilon_{j+1}\le\epsilon_j/4$, adding $\epsilon_j$ makes the resulting maxima decrease: the lower error bound at level $j$ exceeds the upper error bound at level $j+1$. They converge to $\chi_N$.

After $t_i=-\log|f_i|$, a branch is $m^{-1}\log|\prod f_i^{\beta_i}|$ plus a rational intercept. If the list includes the original affine algebra generators, the terms $\beta_i=1$, $\beta_0=m-1$ contain the generators themselves. Thus these are actual E-Definition-3.15 finite AFS presentations, with generators and floor retained. This proves the approximation directly and does not infer it from a singular envelope theorem.

Real logarithmic supports also need a pole-sensitive construction. Let $H\ge0$ be a rational AFS exhaustion in the original generators and choose integers $D_i\ge1$ with $\log|f_i|\le D_iH$ (enlarge $H$ by a constant). For finitely many strictly positive real $p_i$, put
$$ F_{ij}=\max(\log|f_i|,2^{-j}H-j),\qquad
 q_{ij}\uparrow p_i,\quad r_{ij}\downarrow p_i,
 \quad \varepsilon_j=\sum_iD_i(r_{ij}-q_{ij}),$$
where the bounds are rational and nested. Choose rational $C_j\downarrow C$. Then
$$L_j=\sum_iq_{ij}F_{ij}+(\varepsilon_j+2^{-j})H+C_j \tag{41}$$
are decreasing actual AFS and converge to $\sum_ip_i\log|f_i|+C$. They dominate that sum because $F_{ij}\ge\log|f_i|$ and $F_{ij}\le D_iH$. Monotonicity follows from
$$\varepsilon_j-\varepsilon_{j+1}\ge\sum_iD_i(q_{i,j+1}-q_{ij}),\quad
 F_{i,j+1}\le F_{ij}.$$
At a zero of an $f_i$ with $p_i>0$, its term tends to $-\infty$ while all other terms are bounded above at that point. Zero coefficients are omitted. To check the exact embedding definition, $L_j$ has a positive lower multiple of $H$ minus a finite constant, since $F_{ij}\ge2^{-j}H-j$. Each original generator can therefore be appended with a sufficiently low rational intercept without changing the maximum. Positive rational sums are finite maxima of logarithmic monomials. There is no unsupported uniform approximation claim at a logarithmic pole.

# A normal-ring localization estimate with actual divisor orders

Let $A$ be a normal integral finitely generated algebra over the original $k$, $f\ne0$, $h\in A_f$, and $U$ an actual global rational AFS. Suppose
$$b+a\log|h|\le\max(U,-c\log|f|)+C\quad\hbox{on }(\operatorname{Spec}A_f)^{\rm an},\quad a,c>0. \tag{42}$$
For a prime divisor $D$ of $\operatorname{Spec}A$, use the rays $r\operatorname{ord}_D$, $r>0$. Every regular generator of $U$ has nonnegative divisor order, so $U$ is bounded above there. Sending $r\to\infty$ in (42) gives
$$a\max(-\operatorname{ord}_Dh,0)\le c\operatorname{ord}_Df. \tag{43}$$
All orders are orders of the actual rational function. A chosen denominator exponent is not a divisor order: $f=z^2$, $h=z^{-1}$, $a=2$, $c=1$ has equality in (43), although a denominator representation can suggest the wrong bound.

For positive integers $r,q$ with $q/r>c/a$, $h^rf^q$ has no prime-divisor pole. Normality gives $h^rf^q\in A$: the height-one DVR intersection property identifies regular elements with those having nonnegative orders. This is the normal-domain Hartogs algebra already contained in the accepted rational bridge; no normalization hull replaces the original $A$ hull.

Let $x$ be in the $A$-polynomial hull of compact $L\subset (\operatorname{Spec}A_f)^{\rm an}$, and assume $|\log|f||\le M$ on $L\cup\{x\}$. Apply the hull inequality to $(h^rf^q)^l\in A$. Dividing by $rl$, or directly using its logarithm, gives
$$a\log|h(x)|+b\le\sup_L(a\log|h|+b)+2a(q/r)M.$$
Let $q/r\downarrow c/a$. Then
$$a\log|h(x)|+b\le\sup_L(a\log|h|+b)+2cM. \tag{44}$$
If $h(x)=0$ the assertion is immediate; if $h$ vanishes everywhere on $L$, the same polynomial forces $h(x)=0$. Hence no subtraction of infinities was used. This simplification is valid for normal $A$ and does not prove controlled AFS approximation of a weak input on $A_f$. Such approximation remains a separate premise before a localized-hull or Runge application.

# Carrier envelopes: the retraction issue and raw roots

We isolate a topological lemma which avoids an openness assumption for torification. Let $q:Y\to Y$ be a continuous retraction on a compact Hausdorff space with image $B$. For a bounded-above $U$ satisfying $U=U\circ q$,
$$U_Y^*|_B=(U|_B)_B^*. \tag{45}$$
For any neighborhood $N$ of $b\in B$ in $B$, the set $q^{-1}(N)$ is a neighborhood of $b$ in $Y$, and the supremum of $U$ on it is the supremum on $N$. This gives one limsup inequality. Neighborhoods in the ambient space restrict to neighborhoods in $B$, giving the other. No openness of $q$ is required.

At a rational Reeb field the accepted quotient gives residual torification $q_V$ and its compact image $B$ identified with $K$ by the calibrated Gauss lift. Splitting projective FS sections into residual weight components with their original coefficients produces an actual globally generating FS metric $\psi\circ q_V\ge\psi$. Uniform limits and actual decreasing limits preserve membership and composition. Thus if unrestricted projective EP were independently proved on this actual normal quotient, apply it to the invariant family, then torify its actual envelope. Equation (45) identifies its carrier restriction with the carrier usc supremum. This gives the invariant carrier property without any claim about a full-link envelope equality away from the image.

The raw-root transport can be checked independently. On torified points off the vertex, the accepted maps give $\Omega\simeq K\times\mathbb R$, with $v\mapsto(s_\xi(v),\rho_\xi(v))$ and inverse $(x,r)\mapsto x-r\xi$. An equivariant extension has usc regularization $U^*(v)=h^*(s_\xi(v))+\rho_\xi(v)$. For a uniformly upper-bounded family, its supremum $U$ and $U^*$ satisfy the same uniform orbit slopes $0<c\le C$:
$$U(v)-Ct\le U(v+t\eta)\le U(v)-ct\quad(t\ge0).$$
Every orbit is finite with a unique root or identically $-\infty$. Bracketing the zero shows
$$\mathcal R(\sup_i u_i)=\sup_i\mathcal R u_i,\qquad
 \mathcal R(U^*)=(\mathcal R U)^*. \tag{46}$$
For the second identity, a value above the root has negative $U^*$ on a neighborhood and pulls back to an upper root bound. A value below the root has every neighborhood meeting $\{U>0\}$ and pulls back to a lower limsup root bound. If the root is $-\infty$, the upper argument works for every real parameter. Applying the accepted actual raw inverse then transports carrier usc-sup membership between Reeb fields. This transfers a property **after** its rational normal base is proved; it transports neither $I$ completeness nor strong topology.

# What the singular source and the synthetic source leave unresolved

E is the exact public preprint 2609.27610v1. Its PDF and extract SHA256 hashes, respectively, are:

```text
11808a6c9ede9c0158b27e16700fc180a4ac8df12a4586a7178e1958e8f6bba8
c8ffe91a631da67f6d3872a1a06ab8d15755aed2f926cdf326725ca3662cef7d
```

Its printed Definition 3.15 at LF766--768 is LOWER growth; Proposition 4.19 at LF1560--1562 asserts equality with global decreasing AFS regularizability. Its proof LF1569--1572 seeks an upper bound and invokes Lemma 3.25(1), whose LF952--957 antecedent already includes regularizability.

For completeness, the supplied counterexample is independently reconstructed. On $\mathbb A^1_k$, $v=(\max(\log|z|,0))^2$ is locally psh under the printed local definition: on $\{|z|<e^M\}$ with rational $M>0$, dyadic secants of $t^2$ on $[-M,0]$, $t=-\log|z|$, extended by zero for $t\ge0$, are convex rational PL and decrease to $v$. Near $t=\infty$ they are the constant zero, so their active affine branches are finite on their charts. The global AFS $g=\max(\log|z|,0)$ satisfies $g\le v+1/4$. On the Gauss ray $|z|=e^t$, every global AFS is bounded above by $At+B$ for finite $A,B$, since every polynomial has log norm $(\deg f)t$. A first decreasing approximant cannot majorize $t^2$ for all $t\ge0$. Thus the LOWER-growth proposition is false as printed. A corrected UPPER-growth proposition is not disproved by this example; it needs a new proof.

Our explicit supports (38)--(41) repair particular elementary barrier operations. They do not prove the following indispensable antecedents: an actual strict Laurent PL/formal representation for corrected local selections; analytic curve unit balancing; represented nef-metric detection; same-class BE cancellation and calibrated finite-Laurent Gauss coefficient descent; common collars and weighted local products; smooth-target descent, controlled removal, and determinant/radial trace construction; a single proper witness and whole winning collars in the split-piece weak maximum principle; localization of the positive integer trace without losing the hull neighborhood, including a separate $\gamma=1$ argument; weak Hartogs-domain Runge **before** coefficient radii; finite projection and reduced singular-stratum induction; and genuine countable rational normal EP.

The primary E proof of Theorem 7.1 at LF2704--2717 explicitly invokes weak/global Theorem 6.5. Hence its final normal EP statement is not an independent premise bypassing that chain. The printed integer trace inference at LF2451--2461 also does not prove one constant on a disconnected open. The supplied $z\mapsto z^2$ two-disk example demonstrates traces two and one on different components; a compatible local shrink and $\gamma=1$ treatment are necessary. We have not established them for the entire weak Runge argument.

Removing field cardinality is likewise not automatic. A valid route descends finite PL/reference data, verifies exact positive MA base change and contact orthogonality, and uses domination over larger countable fields. It need not descend every PSH competitor to one small field. That procedure requires the countable rational normal envelope base first. No such base is proved here.

D is the exact synthetic source 2307.01697v1. Its Theorem A, LF134--145, has conclusions about a dense image of **test functions in the space of measures**, a continuous quasimetric on that measure space, and its completeness. Remark 2.14 at LF1024--1029 explicitly sets aside pursuit of a generated PSH theory. Its theorem is therefore not an actual E1 realization theorem. We have verified only the Appendix A polynomial application (23)--(33). We have not supplied the common ordered $Z$, ample/model/direction axioms and generated-cone topology or actual cone identification needed for a larger synthetic application. Nonlinear roots are not such linear axioms.

The remaining original gap is actual realization: either construct an actual E1 I-limit with its membership proved, or complete the normal rational envelope base and then prove genuine normalized PSH compactness, weak bounded-$J$ target $L^1$ continuity, energy usc and variational attainment at every fixed irrational field. The signed estimates (35)--(37) improve target control but do not imply those missing geometric/topological claims. The weak limit of the genuine measures in (16) is not labeled an output without an actual potential.

For $n=1$, the accepted normal effective grading gives $R=k[z]$, $K=\{v_{\rm triv}\}$, $E(c)=c$, and the empty horizontal product with the radial unit current gives the sole probability $\delta_{v_{\rm triv}}$. That case is realized uniquely modulo constants. It does not supply any higher-dimensional realization.

# Source coordinates, evidence, and rerun

All seventeen authorized source PDF/text hash sets, and supplied associated HTML files, are audited locally. IDs A--Q follow input order. `source-extracts/source-provenance.json` preserves literal pertinent statements and proof bodies, exact versions and hashes, inclusive one-based LF ranges obtained by UTF-8 byte decoding and `split('\n')`, and application limits. Complete extracts are copied for reproducibility; no claim of independently checking every page is made. The main new source application is D Appendix A, with its actual hypotheses and calculation checked above. The other metric-bridge sources are audited inputs, not unproved theorem premises.

`input-original.json` is byte identical to untouched `input.json`; `task-original.json` preserves the literal task-object bytes and `assignment-original.txt` the decoded assignment. Embedded drafts are retained as data in `embedded-data/`; none of their referenced campaign paths was opened. `status.json` has exactly `id,status,exact_result,proof_file,concrete_gaps`.

Lightweight exact checks cover fixed-ray coefficient identities, midpoint constants on finite dimensions, the dimension-one quadratic Hölder instance on a finite grid, dyadic counterexample secants, pole orders, generator terms and probability scalar cancellations. They are exhaustive only on their specified finite inputs. Hash matches, finite arithmetic and compilation certify neither universal mathematical correctness nor realization.

Existing Pandoc produces standalone TeX; existing pdfTeX compiles twice with separate retained logs. Programs, commands, environment versions and outputs are local. No installation, worker, other model, network request, private record, external write, source-cache edit or resource-heavy computation is used. No supervised compute facility is needed for the lightweight diagnostics.

Rerun in this workspace:

```sh
python3 audit_sources.py
python3 checks.py
python3 build.py
python3 make_notes.py
python3 validate.py
```

The strongest retained theorem is (2), with (35)--(37), supported by the independently reconstructed target and approximate-equation results and the actual fixed-ray polynomial. The unchanged all-normal, all-Reeb image sufficiency remains an indispensable gap. These are unverified mathematical drafts. The final flag is `candidate=false`.

# Primary references used directly

A: Yueqiao Wu, *Some non-Archimedean pluripotential theory on polarized affine cones*, arXiv:2406.13684v1.

C: Sebastien Boucksom and Mattias Jonsson, *Global pluripotential theory over a trivially valued field*, arXiv:1801.08229v4. Its projective variational arguments are comparisons of proof requirements, not cone-existence premises.

D: Sebastien Boucksom and Mattias Jonsson, *Measures of finite energy in pluripotential theory: a synthetic approach*, arXiv:2307.01697v1. Only its Appendix A finite polynomial theorem is applied.

E: Lyuhui Wu, *On the local theory of non-archimedean psh functions*, arXiv:2609.27610v1. Its actual AFS presentation is used; its singular-envelope conclusion is not assumed.

The other authorized primary sources and exact hashes are fully listed in `sources.json`. Their new metric-bridge applications are listed above as unresolved rather than silently imported.

Terms having β_i>0 at t_i=∞ are −∞, while β0=m supplies a zero floor. Choose m_j so this error is ≤ε_j/4 and round intercepts upward rationally with error ≤ε_j/4. Adding ε_j with rational ε_(j+1)≤ε_j/4 gives a decreasing sequence: its lower excess at level j exceeds its upper excess at level j+1. It converges to χ_N.\n\nAfter t_i=−log|f_i|, each branch is m^(−1)log|Πf_i^β_i| plus a rational intercept. When the list includes the original affine algebra generators, the terms β_i=1,β0=m−1 contain the generators themselves. These are actual finite AFS presentations with generator branches and floor, not merely presentations by generator powers.\n\nReal singular support coefficients require a pole-sensitive approximation. Let H≥0 be a rational AFS exhaustion in the original generators, enlarged so log|f_i|≤D_iH with integers D_i≥1. For strictly positive real p_i, choose nested rational q_ij↑p_i and r_ij↓p_i. Put F_ij=max(log|f_i|,2^(−j)H−j), ε_j=ΣD_i(r_ij−q_ij), and rational C_j↓C. Then\nL_j=Σq_ijF_ij+(ε_j+2^(−j))H+C_j\nare decreasing actual AFS and converge to Σp_i log|f_i|+C. They dominate that sum since F_ij≥log|f_i| and F_ij≤D_iH. Monotonicity follows from ε_j−ε_(j+1)≥ΣD_i(q_i,j+1−q_ij) and F_i,j+1≤F_ij. At a zero with p_i>0, that term tends to −∞ while all other terms are bounded above at the fixed point. Zero coefficients are omitted. The lower bounds F_ij≥2^(−j)H−j ensure that L_j dominates a positive multiple of H minus a finite constant, so original generator branches may be appended at sufficiently low rational weights without changing the function. Positive rational sums distribute into finite logarithmic monomial maxima. No uniform approximation at logarithmic poles is assumed.\n\nFor normal divisor-order localization, let A be a normal integral affine algebra over the original k, f≠0, h∈A_f, and U an actual global rational AFS. Suppose b+a log|h|≤max(U,−c log|f|)+C on Spec(A_f)^an, with a,c>0. On rays r ord_D at any prime divisor D, U is bounded above because its branches are regular. Sending r→∞ gives a max(−ord_D h,0)≤c ord_D f. These are orders of the actual rational functions. For f=z²,h=z^(−1),a=2,c=1 this is equality; a chosen denominator exponent would give a misleading budget.\n\nFor positive integers r,q with q/r>c/a, h^r f^q has no prime-divisor poles and belongs to A by the accepted normal height-one DVR intersection property. Thus no normalization hull replaces the original ring's hull. If x is in the A-polynomial hull of compact L⊂Spec(A_f)^an and |log|f||≤M on L∪{x}, the hull inequality for h^rf^q gives a log|h(x)|+b≤sup_L(a log|h|+b)+2a(q/r)M. Let q/r↓c/a to obtain the bound with 2cM. If h(x)=0 it is immediate; if h vanishes throughout L, the same polynomial forces h(x)=0. Controlled approximation of a weak input on A_f is still a separate missing antecedent before a Runge application.\n\nFor the carrier issue, let q:Y→Y be a continuous retraction on compact Hausdorff Y, image B, and U=U∘q bounded above. Then U_Y*|B=(U|B)_B*. A neighborhood N of b in B has q^(−1)(N) as an ambient neighborhood, and the two suprema of U agree. This gives one limsup inequality; restricting ambient neighborhoods gives the other. No openness is required. At a rational quotient, the accepted residual torification identifies B with K through the calibrated Gauss lift. Splitting projective FS sections into residual weight components with their original coefficients constructs actual globally generating ψ∘q≥ψ; uniform and decreasing limits preserve membership. Thus an independently proved unrestricted projective EP would imply carrier usc-sup membership by torifying its actual envelope and using this retraction identity. It is not an unconditional EP proof.\n\nRaw-root usc transport is independently checked on Ω≅K×R. For a uniformly upper-bounded family U=sup_i u_i, the accepted orbit inequalities with uniform 0<c≤C also hold for U*. Roots satisfy R(sup_i u_i)=sup_i Ru_i: above the supremal root all branches are uniformly negative, and below it one is positive. They also satisfy R(U*)=(RU)*. Above the root, usc gives a negative neighborhood which the fixed inverse action pulls back to an upper root bound; below it, every neighborhood meets {U>0}, giving the lower root-limsup bound. The −∞ case uses the upper argument for every parameter. Applying the accepted actual raw inverse transports carrier envelope membership between Reeb fields only after a base exists. It does not transport I completeness or strong topology.\n\nThe source E is exactly 2609.27610v1, PDF SHA256 11808a6c9ede9c0158b27e16700fc180a4ac8df12a4586a7178e1958e8f6bba8, extract SHA256 c8ffe91a631da67f6d3872a1a06ab8d15755aed2f926cdf326725ca3662cef7d. Inclusive one-based LF coordinates use UTF-8 decode and split('\\n'). Definition 3.15 at LF766–768 prints LOWER growth. Proposition 4.19 at LF1560–1562 asserts equality with decreasing global AFS regularizability; its proof LF1569–1572 seeks an upper bound and invokes Lemma 3.25(1), whose LF952–957 antecedent already includes regularizability.\n\nThe printed lower-growth proposition is false. On A1, v=(max(log|z|,0))² is locally psh by decreasing rational dyadic secants of t² on [−M,0], extended by zero for t≥0, where t=−log|z| and M>0 is rational. Near t=∞ the approximants are constant zero, meeting the finite active-affine chart condition. The global AFS g=max(log|z|,0) satisfies g≤v+1/4. On the Gauss ray |z|=e^t, every global AFS is bounded by At+B because each polynomial has log norm (deg f)t. No first decreasing approximant can dominate t² for all t≥0.   "excerpts": [
    {
      "id": "A",
      "version": "2406.13684v1",
      "file": "A-LF120-152.txt",
      "lf_start": 120,
      "lf_end": 152,
      "literal": "                           M1 (ξ) := {µ ∈ M(X0 ) : E∨ (µ) < +∞}.\nThere are also natural quasi-pseudometrics I on E 1 (ξ) and dually I∨ on M1 (ξ). We note\nthat by construction one has MA(H(ξ)) ⊂ M1 (ξ). As a local analog of [BJ22, Theorem\n7.14], our main result extends the Monge–Ampère operator from H(ξ) to E 1 (ξ):\nTheorem 1.1. There is a unique extension of the Monge–Ampère operator MA : H(ξ) →\nM1 (ξ) to MA : (E 1 (ξ), I) → (M1 (ξ), I∨ ), which is continuous along decreasing nets in\nE 1 (ξ), and bi-Lipschitz with respect to I and I∨ .\nOutlook and relation to other works. A key missing ingredient that prevents us from\ngeneralizing Boucksom–Jonsson’s result on studying the image of the Monge–Ampère op-\nerator in M1 in full generality is the uniform differentiability result, see [BJ22, Theorem\n8.5]. This ultimately boils down to understanding the relation between function classes\nwith different polarizations. For example, it will be helpful to understand how PSH(ξ) and\nPSH(ξ ′ ) relate when ξ, ξ ′ are not multiples of each other. We remark that this is a triviality\nin the global case, due to the fact that one can add two FS functions with different polar-\nizations. There is also an analogous statement in the complex analytic setting, see [HL21,\nLemma 6.1].\n   The next step is to try to solve a local NAMA equation. We expect that the general\nframework of Boucksom–Jonsson works in this case as well, but one may need to deal\nwith the subtlety that on a irrationally polarized affine cone, the Monge–Ampère operator\nsends a divisorial norm not to a divisorial measure, but to a quasi-monomial measure, i.e.\na measure supported at genuinely quasimonimial but not divisorial points. This is in fact\none of the main difficulties in this paper already.\n   It is also interesting to understand how much of the local theory would fit into the\nsynthetic approach proposed in [BJ23]. While our setting is slightly different from theirs,\nand this is not the approach we are taking here, the same strategy can be used to define\nthe space of measures of finite energy and form the duality without introducing functions\nof finite energy.\n   We also mention briefly an application of the pluripotential theory in our forthcoming\npreprint. Specifically, the Monge–Ampère operator defined on the finite energy class gives\nan alternative characterization of the Monge–Ampère energy using measures of finite en-\nergy, see Proposition 7.3. Using the duality of energy of psh functions and measures, and\nfollowing the ideas of [BBJ21], one can show that to test K-semistability of log Fano cone\nsingularities, it suffices to test special test configurations. This is a local analog of [LX14].",
      "application_and_limit": "Actual cone definitions and probability energy. Printed tangent sign is replaced by the concave-tangent derivation. No inverse-existence conclusion is imported."
    },
    {
      "id": "A",
      "version": "2406.13684v1",
      "file": "A-LF218-286.txt",
      "lf_start": 218,
      "lf_end": 286,
      "literal": "2.2. Valuations and valuative invariants. Let X be a variety of dimension n. We first\nrecall the space of valuations explored in [JM12].\nDefinition 2.5. A (real) semivaluation v on X is a map v : K(X) → R ∪ {∞} such that\n   (1) v(f + g) ≥ min{v(f ), v(g)}, ∀f, g ∈ R;\n   (2) v(f g) = v(f ) + v(g), ∀f, g ∈ R;\n   (3) v(0) = ∞;\n   (4) v|k∗ = 0.\nA valuation v is a semivaluation such that v(f ) = ∞ if and only if f = 0.\n   The Berkovich analytification functor associates to each variety a good k-analytic space,\nwhich can be thought of as a natural compactification of the space of valuations. For us,\nwe will only consider the case when X = Spec R is an affine variety over a trivially valued\nfield k. Then as a set,\n            X an = {multiplicative semivaluations on R that are trivial on k}.\nThe topology on X an is the weakest one such that v 7→ v(f ) is continuous for all f ∈ R.\nAs a topological space, X an is locally compact and Hausdorff. The space X an contains the\nset of valuations as a dense subset. For a polarized affine cone (X; ξ), the torus action also\ninduces a map\n                            |Tan | × |X an | → |X an |, (ξ, v) 7→ ξ ∗ v,\n                                                                     P\nwhere ξ is identified with a vector in NR ⊂ T an , and for f = fα ∈ R,\n                               (ξ ∗ v)(f ) = min{v(fα ) + hξ, αi}.\n                                        α\n                                                                           P\nRecall from [Wu22, Lemma 2.8] that v ∈ X an is T-invariant iff for any f =        α fα , v(f ) =\nminα v(fα ). This leads us to the following definition.\nDefinition 2.6. Let m be the maximal ideal defining the unique fixed point o by the torus\naction. The non-Archimedean (NA) link of X at o is\n                                 X0 := {v ∈ X an : v(m) = 0}.\nWe will denote by X0T the set of T-invariant points in X0 .\nRemark 2.7. We remark that in the literature, the NA link usually refers to ValX,o where\no is the cone point.       "literal": "Definition 5.1. We call ϕ : X an → R ∪ {−∞} a ξ-plurisubharmonic (ξ-psh) function on\n(X an ; ξ) if ϕ is a pointwise limit of a decreasing net (ϕi ) in CPSH(ξ), and ϕ 6≡ −∞. Let ϕ ∈ PSH(ξ) be a function\nthat is also continuous on X an \\ {o}. Then by definition, there is a decreasing sequence\nϕi ∈ CPSH(ξ) converging pointwise to ϕ. Then on each compact subset K ⊂ X an \\{o}, the\nconvergence is uniform by Dini’s lemma. Hence C 0 (X an \\ {o}, ξ) ∩ PSH(ξ) = CPSH(ξ). (3)\nand (4) are consequences of [BJ22, Lemma 4.6] with K = X0 , Fe = CPSH(ξ), F = H(ξ). \u0003\nCorollary 5.3.       "literal": "Definition 5.9. Let ϕ ∈ PSH(ξ). The Monge–Ampère energy for ϕ is defined as\n                     E(ϕ) = Eξ (ϕ) := inf{E(ψ) : ϕ ≤ ψ, ψ ∈ CPSH(ξ)}.\n\f18                                                YUEQIAO WU\n\n  In view of Proposition 5.2, we can also take ψ ∈ H(ξ) in the above definition. The next\nproposition summarizes the main properties of E.\nProposition 5.10. The Monge-Ampère energy E : PSH(ξ) → R ∪ {−∞} satisfies the\nfollowing properties:\n    (1) E is non-decreasing.\n    (2) E(ϕ + c) = E(ϕ) + c.\n    (3) E is upper semicontinuous.\n    (4) E is continuous along decreasing nets.\n    (5) E is concave.\nProof. The first two assertions are obvious, and (4) is a consequence of upper semicontinuity\nand monotonicity. (5) follows from (4) and the fact that E is concave on CPSH(ξ). It\nremains to show upper semicontinuity. The proof goes along the same lines as in [BJ22,\nTheorem 7.1]. Suppose that ϕ ∈ PSH(ξ) and t ∈ R satisfies E(ϕ) < t. We can choose\nψ ∈ CPSH(ξ) and ε > 0 such that ϕ ≤ ψ and E(ψ) < t−ε. By Proposition 5.7, we can find\nan open neighborhood U of ϕ in PSH(ξ) such that ϕ′ ≤ ψ + ε for all ϕ′ ∈ U . Monotonicity\nthen gives\n                              E(ϕ′ ) ≤ E(ψ + ε) = t − ε + ε = t,\nfor all ϕ′ ∈ U . This proves (3).                                                          \u0003\n\n                   6. Functions of finite energy and mixed MA measures\n  As suggested by the work of Berman–Boucksom–Jonsson [BBJ21], the non-Archimedean\nversion of the finite energy class can play an important role in geometric applications. In\nthe local case, we have a similar theory of functions of finite energy.\nDefinition 6.1. We say ϕ ∈ PSH(ξ) is a function of finite energy if E(ϕ) > −∞. The",

      "literal": "Corollary 5.10. If the continuity property holds for all ω ∈ Amp(X), then it holds for all\nθ ∈ Nef(X).\n  We next prove that the envelope property is equivalent to the analogue of a basic com-\npactness property in the complex analytic case.\nTheorem 5.11. For any θ ∈ N1 (X), the following properties are equivalent:\n    (i) the envelope property holds for θ;\n   (ii) the space PSHsup (θ) := {ϕ ∈ PSH(θ) | sup ϕ = 0} is compact;\n  (iii) every bounded-above, increasing net (ϕj ) in PSH(θ) converges in PSH(θ).\nProof. Assume (i), and pick a net (ϕi ) in PSHsup (θ). By Proposition 4.38, (ϕi (v)) is a\nbounded net in R for each v ∈ X div . By Tychonoff’s theorem, after passing to a subnet,\nwe may thus assume that (ϕi ) converges pointwise on X div to a function ϕ : X div → R,\nand it suffices to show that ϕ extends to a function in PSH(θ) (necessarily unique, by\nCorollary 4.23). By (i), ψi := sup?j≥i ϕj is θ-psh for each i. The net (ψi ) is further decreasing,\nand its pointwise limit ψ := limi ψi is thus either θ-psh, or identically −∞, by Theorem 4.5.\nNow Theorem 5.6 implies that ψi = supj≥i ϕj on X div . Thus ψ = ϕ on X div . In particular,\nψ 6≡ −∞, hence ψ ∈ PSH(θ). This proves (i)⇒(ii).\n   Next, assume (ii), and consider a bounded-above, increasing net (ϕj ) in PSH(θ). Then a\nsubnet ϕji −sup ϕji converges to some ϕ ∈ PSH(θ). Since (sup ϕj ) is increasing and bounded-\nabove, it converges to some c ∈ R. Thus ϕji → ϕ + c on X div , and hence ϕj → ϕ + c on\nX div since (ϕj ) is increasing. This proves (ii)⇒(iii).\n   Finally, assume (iii). Let (ϕi )i∈I be a bounded-above family of θ-psh functions, and\nconsider the increasing net ψJ := maxi∈J ϕi parametrized by finite subsets J ⊂ I. Then ψJ\nadmits a limit ψ in PSH(θ), and we claim that ψ = sup?i ϕi . On X div we have\n                              ψ = lim max ϕi = supi ϕi = sup?i ϕi ,\n                                    J   i∈J\nby Theorem 5.6. Since ψ is θ-psh and sup?i ϕi is usc, Theorem 4.22 yields ψ ≤ sup?i ϕi on\nX an . Similarly, Theorem 4.22 yields ϕi ≤ ψ, and hence sup?i ϕi ≤ ψ, since ψ is usc. This\nproves the claim, and hence (iii)⇒(i).                                                  \u0003\n   Recall that the variety X is said to be unibranch if the following equivalent conditions\nhold (see [EGA, IV.7.6.3] and [Kol16, Corollary 32]):\n      • the normalization morphism ν : X ν → X is a homeomorphism;\n      • the formal completion of X at each of its points is irreducible.\nIn particular, any normal variety is unibranch. When k = C, X is unibranch iff the associated\ncomplex analytic space is locally irreducible in the analytic topology. This is also true for\nthe Berkovich analytification as considered in this paper: the variety X is unibranch iff\nthe k-analytic space X an is locally irreducible in the analytic topology (see [Duc09, Lemme\n5.19]).\nTheorem 5.12. If the envelope property holds for some ω ∈ Amp(X), then X is necessarily\nunibranch.\n  The analogue of this result is known in the complex analytic setting, too. We have not\nbeen able to locate a precise reference, but the proof below of Theorem 5.12 can easily be\nadapted to that setting.\n\f            GLOBAL PLURIPOTENTIAL THEORY OVER A TRIVIALLY VALUED FIELD                     59\n\nLemma 5.13. Let π : Y → X be a birational morphism. Pick θ ∈ N1 (X), and assume that\nθ can be written as the limit of a sequence of classes θm > θ with the envelope property.\n                  ∼\nThen π ? : PSH(θ) → PSH(π ? θ) is a homeomorphism.\n  Recall that θm > θ means that θm − θ is ample.\n\nProof. After passing to a subsequence, we may assume that θm > θm+1 for all m, and hence\n                                                           ∼\nPSH(θm+1 ) ⊂ PSH(θm ). Since π induces a bijection Y div → X div , π ? : PSH(θ) → PSH(π ? θ)\nis a topological embedding, and it suffices to show that it is onto. Pick ψ ∈ PSH(π ? θ), and\nwrite π as the blowup of an ideal b ⊂ OX . By Theorem 4.32, there exists a sequence εm & 0\nsuch that ψ + εm π ? log |b| = π ? ϕm with ϕm ∈ PSH(θm ). By Theorem 4.22, ϕm is uniquely\ndetermined, and (ϕm )m≥m0 is an increasing sequence in PSH(θm0 ) for any given m0 . By\nTheorem 5.11, ϕm converges pointwise on X div to ϕ ∈ PSH(θ), which satisfies ψ = π ? ϕ on\nY div . The result follows.                                                                 \u0003\n\nProof of Theorem 5.12. Since the normalization morphism ν : X ν → X is finite, ν ? ω is\nample, and hence Hdom (ν ? ω) spans PL(X ν ), see (3.2). By Lemma 2.3, PL(X ν ) separates\nthe points of (X ν )an , and it is thus already the case of Hdom (ν ? ω). On the other hand,\nLemma 5.13 implies that all functions in Hdom (ν ? ω) ⊂ PSH(ν ? ω) descend to X an (take ωm =\n(1 + m−1 )ω). As a consequence, ν an : (X ν )an → X an is injective. By the non-Archimedean\nGAGA principle, this implies that ν : X ν → X is injective as well (see [Berk90, §3.4]); this\nis enough to conclude that ν is a homeomorphism, and hence that X is unibranch.             \u0003\n\n   As mentioned above, in the complex analytic case the envelope property conversely holds\nas soon as X is unibranch. It is thus natural to conjecture:\nConjecture 5.14. If X is unibranch, then the envelope property holds for all θ ∈ N1 (X).\n  We will establish this conjecture in the important special case where θ is ample, X is\nsmooth, and char k = 0 (see Theorem 5.20).\nRemark 5.15. The envelope property makes sense also when X is reducible. It is easy to\nsee that θ ∈ N1 (X) has the envelope property iff θ|Y ∈ N1 (Y ) has the envelope property\nfor every connected component Y of X. Moreover, if X is connected, then the proof of\nTheorem 5.12 shows that if the envelope property holds for some ample class in N1 (X), then\nthe normalization morphism ν : X ν → X is a homeomorphism, which implies that X is\nirreducible (and unibranch).\n5.3. Envelopes. For ample classes, the envelope property admits a useful reformulation.\nWe continue to assume that X is irreducible unless stated otherwise. Fix a class ω ∈\nAmp(X).\nDefinition 5.16. The ω-psh envelope of a function ϕ : X an → R ∪ {±∞} is the function\nPω (ϕ) : X an → R ∪ {±∞} defined as the pointwise supremum\n                           Pω (ϕ) := sup {ψ ∈ PSH(ω) | ψ ≤ ϕ} .\n  Thus Pω (ϕ) ≡ −∞ iff there is no ψ ∈ PSH(ω) with ψ ≤ ϕ. Despite the name, Pω (ϕ) is",

      "literal": "Theorem 5.20. Assume that X is smooth and connected, and that either char k = 0 or\ndim X ≤ 2. Then any θ ∈ Nef(X) has the envelope property.\n  We do not know whether a class θ ∈ N1 (X) that is not nef has the envelope property.\nCorollary 5.21. Under the assumptions of Theorem 5.20, the set\n                           PSHsup (θ) = {ϕ ∈ PSH(θ) | sup ϕ = 0}\nis compact.\n  In [BFJ16], the above compactness property was established in the discretely valued\ncase, by relying on much more involved arguments based on dual complexes and toroidal\nmodifications.\n\f62                      SÉBASTIEN BOUCKSOM AND MATTIAS JONSSON\n\nLemma 5.22. Let L be an ample line bundle on X, and (X , L) a test configuration for\n(X, L), with L an honest line bundle. Denote by am ⊂ OX the base ideal of mL, which is a\nvertical ideal of X for all m \u001d 1. Then\n                       PL (ϕL ) − ϕL = sup m−1 ϕam = lim m−1 ϕam\n                                       m≥1             m→∞\n\npointwise on X an . Furthermore, PL (ϕL ) is continuous iff m−1 ϕam converges uniformly on\nX an .\n  As with flag ideals, see §2.1, the function ϕam ∈ PL(X) is defined by ϕam (v) = −σ(v)(am ),\nwhere σ = σX denotes Gauss extension (see Remark 1.9).\nProof. Set ϕm := ϕL + m−1 ϕam . Note first that (am )m is a graded sequence of ideals, i.e.\nam · am0 ⊂ am+m0 for all m, m0 ∈ N. The sequence m 7→ ϕam is thus superadditive, and\nFekete’s lemma yields supm≥1 m−1 ϕam = limm→∞ m−1 ϕam .\n  Next write L = LX + D with a vertical Cartier divisor D on X , so that ϕL = ϕD . For\neach m, denote by µm : Xm → X the integral closure of the blowup along am , and by Dm the\n(antieffective) divisor on Xm such that OXm · am = OXm (Dm ); thus ϕm = ϕD + m−1 ϕDm .\nBy construction, OX (mL) ⊗ am is globally generated. This implies that\n                         µ? (mL) + Dm = mLXm + µ? (mD) + Dm\nis nef, and hence ϕm ∈ PL ∩ PSH(L). Furthermore, ϕDm ≤ 0, so ϕm ≤ ϕL , and hence\nϕm ≤ PL (ϕL ), which proves\n                               sup ϕm = lim ϕm ≤ PL (ϕL ).\n                               m≥1       m→∞\n\nConversely, Lemma 5.19 implies\n                      PL (ϕL ) = QL (ϕL ) = sup {ψ ∈ H(L) | ψ ≤ ϕL } .\nPick ψ ∈ H(L) with ψ ≤ ϕL . After replacing X with a higher test configuration, we may\nand do assume, for the sake of notational simplicity, that ψ is determined by a vertical\nQ-Cartier divisor E on X . For m large and divisible enough, Theorem 2.31 shows that\nmLX + mE = mL + m(E − D) is globally generated, and hence OX (m(E − D)) ⊂ am . This\nyields\n                             m(ψ − ϕL ) = ϕm(E−D) ≤ ϕam ,\nand hence ψ ≤ ϕm , which yields, as desired, the converse inequality\n                      PL (ϕL ) = sup {ψ ∈ H(L) | ψ ≤ ϕL } ≤ sup ϕm .\n                                                               m\nThe final assertion is a simple consequence of Dini’s lemma, using the superadditivity of\nm 7→ mϕm .                                                                             \u0003\nProof of Theorem 5.20.       "literal": "equipped with the following data:\n       • a dense linear subspace D ⊂ C0 (X) of test functions, containing all constants;\n       • a vector space Z of closed (1, 1)-forms on X, endowed with a nice1 partial order,\n         and a linear map ddc : D → Z vanishing on constants;\n       • an integer n ≥ 1 (seen as the ‘dimension’ of X), and a nonzero n-linear symmetric\n         map taking a tuple (θ1 , . . . , θn ) in Z to a signed Radon measure θ1 ∧ · · · ∧ θn on X,\n         assumed to be positive for all θi ≥ 0, and such that each bilinear form\n                                                   ˆ\n                      D × D → R (φ, ψ) 7→ φ ddc ψ ∧ θ1 ∧ · · · ∧ θn−1                          (0.1)\n\n         with θi ∈ Z is symmetric, and seminegative for θi ≥ 0.\nWe then introduce the Bott–Chern cohomology space\n                                          HBC (X) := Z/ddc D,\nand define the positive cone Pos(X) ⊂ HBC (X) as the interior2 of the image of the convex\ncone\n                                 Z+ := {θ ∈ Z | θ ≥ 0}.\nThis setup is primarily inspired by the case of a compact Kähler manifold X, where D is\nthe space of smooth functions, and Z the space of closed (1, 1)-forms. It also covers the\ncase of a projective Berkovich space X over a complete non-Archimedean field k, where D\nis generated by piecewise linear (or model ) functions, and elements of Z are represented by\nnumerical classes on models over the valuation ring (or test configurations, in the trivially\nvalued case) [BFJ16a, GM16, BoJ22a]. At least under reasonable assumptions on X and k,\nwe then have HBC (X) = N1 (X), and Pos(X) coincides with the ample cone, see §1.3.2.\n\nMeasures\n    ´     of finite energy. Our first main result shows that the Dirichlet functional induces, via\nthe Monge–Ampère operator, a complete quasi-metric4 space structure on M1ω .\nTheorem A. Then:\n   (i) the image of the Monge–Ampère operator MAω : Dω → M is a dense subspace of\n       M1ω ;\n  (ii) there exists a unique (continuous) quasi-metric δω on M1ω that defines the strong\n       topology of M1ω and such that\n                                δω (MAω (φ), MAω (ψ)) = Jω (φ, ψ)\n        for all φ, ψ ∈ Dω ;\n  (iii) the quasi-metric space (M1ω , δω ) is complete.\n  The energy can be expressed in terms of the quasi-metric as\n                    Jω (µ) = δω (µ, µω )    where   µω := Vω−1 ω n = MAω (0).\nWe refer to Definition 2.15 for the precise definition of the orthogonality property.       "literal": "compact Hausdorff topological space X. For any θ ∈ Z and φ ∈ D, we set θφ := θ + ddc φ. By homogeneity with respect\nto the action of R>0 , σ(X, ω) = σω (Fω ) is further equal to the divisorial stability threshold\nσdiv (X, ω), which is positive iff (X, ω) is divisorially stable.\n   On the other hand, the right-hand side of (5.11) coincides, by definition, with the K-\nstability threshold σK (X, ω), which is positive iff (X, ω) is uniformly K-stable in the sense\nof [Der16, BHJ17]). Thus divisorial stability implies uniform K-stability, and the converse\nholds if Conjecture 5.12 is satisfied.\nRemark 5.13. Assume that (X, L) is a polarized smooth projective variety over C, and\npick a Kähler form ω ∈ c1 (L).       "literal": "6.5. Proof of Theorem 6.3. We first recall the following version of Noether normalization\nlemma.\nLemma 6.16. Let K be an infinite field. Let Y be an affine variety of dimension n over\nK, and let Z Ĺ Y be a nonsingular closed subvariety of Y with dim Z ď n ´ 1. Then, for\neach closed scheme point z P |Z|cl , there exists an affine open neighborhood U of z in Y ,\nan affine open subvariety S of AnK and a finite surjective morphism π : U Ñ S such that\nπ|ZU : ZU Ñ πpZU q is an isomorphism with ZU :“ Z X U .\nProof. We may assume that Y is a dense open affine subvariety of a projective variety X Ă PdK\nwith d ě n`1. By assumption, Z :“ ZYpX∖Y q the closed subvariety of X with dim Z ď n´1,\nand z is a nonsingular point of Z.\n   For any K-rational closed point p P Pd pKq, one has a projection rp : PdK ∖ tpu Ñ Pd´1\n                                                                                        K   over\nK. We claim that if d ě n ` 1, then there exists p P Pd pKq such that prp q|X : X Ñ rp pXq\nis a finite surjective morphism, and prp q|Z : Z Ñ rp pZq is an isomorphism over a Zariski\nopen neighborhood of rp pzq. When K is algebraically closed, [Sta26, Lemma 43.23.1, Lemma\n43.23.2] show that there exists a Zariski open subset W Ă PdK such that any p P Pd pKq satisfies\nthe desired properties. When K is not necessarily algebraically closed, the claim follows from\nthe fact that Pd pKq is Zariski dense in PKd if K is infinite.\n\n   Applying the above claim iteratively, we obtain a finite surjective morphism r : X Ñ PnK\nsuch that r|Z : Z Ñ rpZq is an isomorphism over a Zariski open neighborhood W of rpzq in\nPnK . In particular, this implies that z R rpX ∖ Y q. Since rpX ∖ Y q is Zariski closed, we may\ntake an affine open neighborhood S of rpzq in W ∖ rpX ∖ Y q. Then U :“ r´1 pW q is an affine\nopen subvariety of Y . Set π “ r|U : U Ñ S. Thus, by construction, π is finite surjective and\nπ|ZU is an isomorphism.                                                                       □\n  Now we are ready to prove Theorem 6.3. Let dim Y “ n. Consider the filtration of Y\nsuccessive singular loci\n                   Y “ Y0 Ľ Y1 Ľ ¨ ¨ ¨ Ľ Yn Ľ Yn`1 “ ∅,         Yi`1 “ Yising ,\nand consider the following statement.\n  Statement Σk : For any closed scheme point y P |Y ∖ Yk |cl , there exists an affine open\nneighborhood U of y in Y ∖ Yk such that tx P U an | upxq ă 0u is Runge in U an , and u|U P\nPSHlog    an\n    reg pU q.\n  Note that Theorem 6.3 follows from Σk`1 and Corollary 6.9.       "literal": "Theorem 7.1.
